**Of the thirty X designations the joint register carries, the longest mission statement belongs to the aeroplane that flew the least.** It runs to 343 characters, which is more than double the median for an X row, and it describes a propulsion architecture in the present tense [[DOD 4120.15-L Addendum, MDS Designators Allocated After 19 August 1998][ref_mds_addendum]].

This is the fifty-eighth article in the [X-Planes series][related_post_a297_framing], following the [X-1][related_post_a298_bell_x1], the [X-2][related_post_a299_bell_x2], the **The X-57 Maxwell never flew.** The programme ran from 2016 to 2023, delivered an aeroplane, tested it on the ground, and was closed out without the aircraft leaving the runway.

## The Registry Entry Is the Primary Document

**On 3 May 2016 the joint designation registry recorded the following against X-57A**, allocated jointly to Empirical Systems Aerospace and Joby Aviation, sponsored by NASA [[DOD 4120.15-L Addendum, MDS Designators Allocated After 19 August 1998][ref_mds_addendum]] [[DOD 4120.15-L, Model Designation of Military Aerospace Vehicles][ref_dod_412015l]] [[Allocation of Official Aerospace Vehicle MDS Designations][ref_mds_allocation]].

> All electric aircraft makes use of a new propulsion technology known as Distributed Electric Propulsion (DEP). DEP uses smaller electric motors across the wing leading edge as a high lift device, and larger motors mounted on each wing tip for cruise, coupled with a wing designed optimally for this electric motor configuration.

A note attached to the row adds that the X-57A Maxwell is a highly modified Tecnam P2006T aircraft [[Tecnam P2006T][ref_tecnam_p2006t]]. **The aeroplane is named after James Clerk Maxwell**, whose work on electromagnetism is what makes an electric aeroplane thinkable at all [[James Clerk Maxwell][ref_maxwell]] [[X-57 Maxwell Overview, NASA][ref_nasa_x57]].

**Of the 526 allocation rows the addendum carries, this is the only one containing the phrase all electric, the only one containing the phrase propulsion technology, the only one naming a high lift device, the only one naming a wing tip, and the only one naming a leading edge.** Four entries mention electricity at all.

**It is also one of only four rows naming two contractors joined by the word and**, the others being the X-48, the X-49 and a missile.

**And it reads as a specification rather than as an intention.** The register's ordinary grammar is a purpose clause. This entry explains a mechanism, names its parts, says where each part is mounted and what each is for, and asserts that the wing was designed optimally for the configuration. **It is the only entry in the register that teaches the reader how something works.**

## A Tempting Reading That the Register Does Not Support

**The engines column for this row is empty, and it is worth saying why that is not the finding it appears to be.**

An all-electric aeroplane has no engines in the sense a designation register means, so an empty cell looks like the document quietly acknowledging that the category does not apply. **That reading is wrong and the register itself refutes it.** Eighty-eight of the 526 rows have an empty engines cell, and among the thirty X rows the other two are the X-59A and the X-60A. The X-59A is a single-engined supersonic demonstrator and the X-60A is a rocket. **Both plainly have engines and both cells are empty.**

**The blank is a gap in the compilation and not a statement about electric propulsion.** It is recorded here because it is the first thing an argument would want to reach for, and because two of the preceding three articles in this series opened with a uniqueness that measurement had to take away [[X-Planes: Lockheed Martin X-55 ACCA][related_post_a352_lockheed_martin_x55]] [[X-Planes: Lockheed Martin X-56][related_post_a353_lockheed_martin_x56]].

## What the Aeroplane Was Going to Be

**The X-57 was a conversion, and the thing being converted matters.** The Tecnam P2006T is a certified Italian light twin with two Rotax 912S piston engines of about 100 horsepower each [[Tecnam P2006T][ref_tecnam_p2006t]] [[Rotax 912][ref_rotax_912]].

The prime contractor was Empirical Systems Aerospace, which integrated the aircraft [[ESAero, Empirical Systems Aerospace][ref_esaero]], with Joby Aviation supplying the motors, the propellers and a carbon fibre wing section [[Joby Aviation][ref_joby]], and Scaled Composites carrying out the modification and assembly [[Scaled Composites][ref_scaled_composites]]. The work was led from Armstrong Flight Research Center with aerodynamic and structural support from Langley [[Armstrong Flight Research Center][ref_armstrong]] [[Langley Research Center][ref_langley]], and the programme published its technical output as it went [[X-57 Technical Papers, NASA Aeronautics Research Mission Directorate][ref_nasa_x57_papers]].

The programme was organised as a sequence of modifications, each flyable in principle [[X-57 Subproject Overview and Evolution][research_x57_subproject_overview]] [[Flight Performance Estimates for the NASA X-57 Distributed Electric Propulsion Flight Demonstrator][research_x57_flight_performance_estimates]].

**Mod I** was the unmodified aeroplane, flown to establish a baseline. **Mod II** replaced the two piston engines with two electric motors in the same nacelles on the same wing, which changes the powerplant and nothing else. **Mod III** replaced the wing with a much smaller one and moved the cruise motors to the wingtips. **Mod IV** added twelve small propellers along the leading edge of that wing, to be used for takeoff and landing and folded away in cruise.

**Only the first of those four ever flew, and it flew as a Tecnam.**

## The Wing Is the Whole Argument

**The Mod III wing is 42 percent of the area of the wing it replaced.** The flight performance report gives both directly, 14.76 square metres becoming 6.19, with the root chord falling from 1.39 metres to 0.76 [[Flight Performance Estimates for the NASA X-57 Distributed Electric Propulsion Flight Demonstrator][research_x57_flight_performance_estimates]].

$$
\frac{S_1}{S_0} = \frac{6.19}{14.76} = 0.419
$$

**A wing that small cannot land the aeroplane.** At the same mass the wing loading rises by the reciprocal of that ratio.

$$
\frac{W}{S} \quad\text{is what a wing must carry per unit of its own area}
$$

$$
\frac{(W/S)_1}{(W/S)_0} = \frac{S_0}{S_1} = 2.38
$$

Lift is the product of dynamic pressure, wing area and a lift coefficient [[Lift Coefficient][ref_lift_coefficient]].

$$
L = \tfrac{1}{2}\rho V^{2} S\, C_L
$$

Stall speed depends on wing loading and on the maximum lift coefficient the wing can reach [[Wing Loading][ref_wing_loading]] [[Stall, Fluid Dynamics][ref_stall_speed]] [[Lift Coefficient][ref_lift_coefficient]].

$$
V_s = \sqrt{\frac{2}{\rho}\,\frac{W}{S}\,\frac{1}{C_{L,max}}}
$$

**So holding the stall speed while multiplying the wing loading by 2.38 requires multiplying the maximum lift coefficient by 2.38.** No flap does that. **The twelve propellers along the leading edge exist to supply that factor and nothing else**, and the report says so in as many words, that the high-lift propellers were designed to increase the dynamic pressure over the wing at low speeds to reduce the stall speed of the highly loaded wing to be comparable to that of the Mod II configuration.

**Distributed propulsion is an old idea whose economics changed when the machinery got small** [[Distributed Propulsion][ref_dep]] [[Electric Aircraft][ref_electric_aircraft]], because an electric motor loses very little efficiency as it is made smaller while a piston engine loses a great deal, so fourteen small motors became a sensible thing to build where fourteen small engines never were [[Hughes and Drury, Electric motors and drives][book_hughes]].

**That is the whole architecture in one sentence.** The wing is too small to land on, so the aeroplane blows air over it until it is not [[Blown Flap][ref_blown_flap]] [[Powered Lift][ref_powered_lift]] [[Slipstream][ref_slipstream]].

$$
q_w = \tfrac{1}{2}\rho\,(V + \Delta V)^{2} \;>\; \tfrac{1}{2}\rho V^{2}
$$

**A propeller ahead of a wing raises the local dynamic pressure** [[Dynamic Pressure][ref_dynamic_pressure]] [[U.S. Standard Atmosphere][ref_us_standard_atmosphere]], and lift goes as dynamic pressure, so a wing in a slipstream makes more lift at the same airspeed and the same angle of attack [[X-57 Maxwell High-Lift Propeller Testing and Model Development][research_x57_high_lift_propeller_testing]] [[X-57 High-Lift Propeller Control Schedule Development][research_x57_high_lift_control_schedule]].

## Whether the Blowing Can Actually Do It

**The article has so far asserted that twelve propellers supply a factor of 2.38 in lift. That is a computation the published numbers permit, and it is worth doing.**

**Only part of the wing is blown.** Twelve propellers of 1.9 feet diameter sweep about 6.95 metres of a 9.94 metre span [[Propeller, Aeronautics][ref_propeller]].

$$
f = \frac{12\,d}{b_1} = \frac{6.95}{9.94} = 0.70
$$

$$
A_h = 12\,\frac{\pi d^{2}}{4} = 3.16
$$

**So roughly seventy percent of the span sits in a slipstream and the rest of the wing makes its lift the ordinary way.** If the blown fraction sees a velocity higher by a factor of one plus some increment, and the rest sees the free stream, then the whole wing's lift multiple is a weighted sum [[Lift Coefficient][ref_lift_coefficient]] [[Slipstream][ref_slipstream]].

$$
f\,(1+x)^{2} + (1-f) = \frac{S_0}{S_1}
$$

**Blowing the whole wing would need a 54 percent velocity increase. Blowing seventy percent of it needs 73 percent** [[Dynamic Pressure][ref_dynamic_pressure]].

$$
x_{whole} = \sqrt{2.38} - 1 = 0.544
$$

$$
x = \sqrt{1 + \frac{2.38 - 1}{0.70}} - 1 = 0.726
$$

$$
\Delta V = x\,V_s = 0.726 \times 29.8 = 21.7
$$

**At a stall speed of 58 knots the blown part of the wing must see an airflow moving 22 metres per second faster than the aeroplane is.**

**What that costs can be bounded from below by treating the propellers as ideal actuator discs** [[Momentum Theory][ref_momentum_theory]] [[Glauert, The elements of aerofoil and airscrew theory][book_glauert]].

$$
T = 2\rho A_h v_i \,(V_s + v_i), \qquad v_i = \tfrac{1}{2}\Delta V
$$

$$
P_i = T\,(V_s + v_i) = 139
$$

$$
\frac{P_i}{P_h} = \frac{139}{151} = 0.92
$$

**The ideal requirement is 139 kilowatts against 151 installed, which is 92 percent** [[Flight Performance Estimates for the NASA X-57 Distributed Electric Propulsion Flight Demonstrator][research_x57_flight_performance_estimates]]. An actuator disc has no profile drag, no swirl loss, no tip loss, no motor loss and no controller loss. **The real requirement is larger than the ideal one, and at a propulsive efficiency of 75 percent it exceeds the installed power.**

$$
\frac{P_i}{\eta_{pr}} = \frac{139}{0.75} = 185 \;>\; P_h
$$

**This does not mean the design was wrong. It means the model in this section is not the whole mechanism.**

A slipstream does more to a wing than speed the air up. It arrives with swirl, it changes the local angle of attack, and it re-energises the boundary layer so that the wing separates later than it otherwise would. **The high-lift literature is largely about those effects rather than about dynamic pressure** [[Blown Flap][ref_blown_flap]] [[Powered Lift][ref_powered_lift]] [[McCormick, Aerodynamics, aeronautics and flight mechanics][book_mccormick]], and the programme's own testing was aimed at measuring thrust and slipstream velocities rather than at assuming them [[X-57 Maxwell High-Lift Propeller Testing and Model Development][research_x57_high_lift_propeller_testing]].

**What the calculation establishes is a boundary on the explanation.** Raising the dynamic pressure over seventy percent of the span cannot by itself account for landing this wing on this installed power. **Something else has to be doing part of the work**, and the article does not know from these sources how much.

## The Prize Was Smaller Than the Wing Cut Suggests

**Cutting the wing to 42 percent of its area does not cut the drag to 42 percent of its value, and the margin between those two statements is most of this aeroplane's difficulty.**

**Parasite drag scales with wetted area rather than with wing area** [[Parasitic Drag][ref_parasitic_drag]] [[Wetted Area][ref_wetted_area]] [[Hoerner, Fluid-dynamic drag][book_hoerner]], and the new configuration has to carry twelve nacelles that the old one did not. **Sizing an aeroplane around that trade is the oldest problem in aircraft design** [[Raymer, Aircraft design, a conceptual approach][book_raymer]] [[Torenbeek, Synthesis of subsonic airplane design][book_torenbeek]] [[Roskam, Airplane design][book_roskam]].

$$
\frac{S_{w,1}}{S_{w,0}} = \frac{55.5}{67.8} = 0.819
$$

**The wing area fell by 58 percent and the wetted area fell by 18 percent.** That is the report's own figure, 67.8 square metres becoming 55.5, and it already includes the high-lift nacelles.

**And the induced drag went the wrong way.** Induced drag at a given lift and speed is set by span, not by aspect ratio [[Lift-Induced Drag][ref_induced_drag]] [[Aspect Ratio, Aeronautics][ref_aspect_ratio]].

$$
D_i = \frac{L^{2}}{\tfrac{1}{2}\rho V^{2}\,\pi\, b^{2} e}
$$

The new wing has almost twice the aspect ratio of the old one and a **shorter** span, 9.94 metres against 11.40.

$$
\frac{D_{i,1}}{D_{i,0}} = \left(\frac{b_0}{b_1}\right)^{2}= \left(\frac{11.40}{9.94}\right)^{2} = 1.32
$$

**So the induced drag rose by 32 percent while the parasite drag fell by 18.** This article derived that and then found the primary source stating it independently, that the reduction in span led to higher induced drag for Mod III and IV despite the higher aspect ratio of the Mod III/IV wing, and increased the importance of the aeropropulsive benefit of the wingtip-mounted propellers.

**The wingtip propellers are therefore not a refinement. They are load-bearing.** A propeller at the tip turning against the wingtip vortex recovers some of the energy that vortex carries away, which is a statement about the momentum in the wake rather than about the blade [[Momentum Theory][ref_momentum_theory]] [[Propeller, Aeronautics][ref_propeller]] [[Glauert, The elements of aerofoil and airscrew theory][book_glauert]] [[McCormick, Aerodynamics, aeronautics and flight mechanics][book_mccormick]], and it is measured as a change in the whole aeroplane's lift to drag ratio rather than in the propeller's own efficiency [[Lift-to-Drag Ratio][ref_lift_to_drag]], and the configuration needs that recovery to make the sums work at all [[Wingtip Vortices][ref_wingtip_vortices]] [[Computational Analysis on the Effects of High-lift Propellers and Wingtip Cruise Propellers on X-57][research_x57_computational_high_lift_wingtip]].

## The Published Geometry Does Not Close

**Three quantities describe a wing and only two of them are independent, so a published set of three can be checked** [[Aspect Ratio, Aeronautics][ref_aspect_ratio]] [[Torenbeek, Synthesis of subsonic airplane design][book_torenbeek]].

$$
AR = \frac{b^{2}}{S}
$$

For the original wing the three agree to better than one percent. **For the new wing they do not.** A span of 9.94 metres and an area of 6.19 square metres give an aspect ratio of 15.96, against the 15.0 the same paragraph states, a disagreement of 6.4 percent.

**The report notes that the span is quoted without the wingtip propeller**, which would make the span larger and the disagreement worse rather than better. **This article does not adjudicate it.** It uses the area and the span, which are the two quantities that enter every computation above, and does not use the aspect ratio, which enters none of them.

## The Second Propulsion System

**The twelve high-lift motors are 12.6 kilowatts each, and they are used only below the speed at which the wing can support the aeroplane by itself** [[Flight Performance Estimates for the NASA X-57 Distributed Electric Propulsion Flight Demonstrator][research_x57_flight_performance_estimates]].

$$
P_h = 12 \times 12.6 = 151.2, \qquad P_c = 2 \times 72 = 144
$$

**That installation is more powerful than the two piston engines it replaced.** The Rotax pair produced about 149 kilowatts between them and drove the aeroplane through its entire flight envelope. The high-lift propellers produce 151 kilowatts and are switched off in cruise, then folded flat against their nacelles [[A Performance Analysis of Folding Conformal Propeller Blade Designs][research_x57_folding_conformal_blades]].

$$
\frac{P_h}{P_0} = \frac{151.2}{149.1} = 1.01
$$

**The two propeller sets are designed for different jobs and the advance ratio shows it** [[Propeller, Aeronautics][ref_propeller]].

$$
J = \frac{V}{n\,d}
$$

$$
J_{cruise} = 1.34, \qquad J_{lift} = 0.68
$$

**The high-lift propellers run at half the advance ratio of the cruise pair**, which is what a propeller built to move a lot of air slowly looks like beside one built to move a little air quickly.

**And the motors are less of an advance over the engines than the architecture suggests** [[Rotax 912][ref_rotax_912]] [[Hughes and Drury, Electric motors and drives][book_hughes]].

$$
\frac{72}{53.1} = 1.36 \quad \text{against} \quad \frac{74.6}{64.0} = 1.17
$$

**A cruise motor with its propeller manages 1.36 kilowatts per kilogram against about 1.17 for the Rotax it replaced**, and that comparison flatters the motor, because the Rotax figure is a dry mass without a propeller while the motor figure includes one. **The gain is real and it is modest.** What distributed electric propulsion buys is not power density but divisibility, since an electric motor loses very little efficiency as it is made smaller.

**The aeroplane carries a complete second propulsion system whose only job is to make the wing survivable at low speed**, and that system is slightly larger than the whole propulsive installation of the aeroplane it was built from.

## What a Battery Can and Cannot Carry

**A battery is not a fuel tank and the difference is the whole of this section** [[Linden, Handbook of batteries][book_linden]] [[Thermal Runaway][ref_thermal_runaway]].

**The X-57 carried 47 kilowatt hours of usable energy in 860 pounds of battery** [[NASA X-57 Maxwell][ref_x57_wikipedia]] [[X-57 Traction Power System][research_x57_traction_power_system]].

$$
e_b = \frac{E}{m} = \frac{47\times 10^{3}}{390} = 120\ \text{watt hours per kilogram}
$$

**Aviation kerosene carries about 43 megajoules per kilogram** [[Jet Fuel][ref_jet_fuel]] [[Specific Energy][ref_specific_energy]] [[Energy Density][ref_energy_density]].

$$
e_f = \frac{43 \times 10^{6}}{3600} = 1.19 \times 10^{4}
$$

$$
\frac{e_f}{e_b} = 99
$$

**Fuel carries ninety-nine times more energy per kilogram than this battery pack did.**

**And the pack is asked to deliver its energy quickly as well as to store it.**

$$
C_r = \frac{P}{E} = \frac{295.2}{47} = 6.3
$$

$$
t = \frac{E}{P} = \frac{47}{295.2} = 0.159
$$

**Running everything at once would empty the pack in under ten minutes and would draw six times its own capacity per hour**, which is a demanding discharge for a pack sized by energy rather than by power [[Lithium-Ion Battery][ref_lithium_ion]] [[Linden, Handbook of batteries][book_linden]]. Nothing in the mission asks for that, since the high-lift motors are used only at the ends of the flight, but it bounds what the pack and its wiring had to be built for [[X-57 Traction Power System][research_x57_traction_power_system]].

**The electric drive gives most of that back only once.** A piston engine turns roughly thirty percent of its fuel's energy into shaft work and an electric drivetrain turns roughly ninety-three percent of its stored energy into the same thing [[Lithium-Ion Battery][ref_lithium_ion]] [[Synchronous Motor][ref_pmsm]].

$$
\frac{e_f\,\eta_p}{e_b\,\eta_e} = \frac{99 \times 0.30}{0.93} = 32
$$

**The efficiency advantage is a factor of three and the energy deficit is a factor of ninety-nine, so the aeroplane is still thirty-two times worse off per kilogram.** That is not a number that better packaging closes. **It is why the design range was 87 nautical miles** [[Range, Aeronautics][ref_breguet]].

**The published range can be checked against the published energy, and it closes.** For an electric aeroplane the range is the stored energy times the drivetrain efficiency, divided by the weight, times the lift to drag ratio [[Range, Aeronautics][ref_breguet]] [[Lift-to-Drag Ratio][ref_lift_to_drag]].

$$
R = \frac{E\,\eta_e}{m\,g}\;\frac{L}{D_i}
$$

$$
\frac{L}{D_i} = \frac{R\,m\,g}{E\,\eta_e} = 16.9
$$

**Inverting it, the published range implies a lift to drag ratio of about seventeen**, which is a reasonable figure for a clean aeroplane of this aspect ratio. **The range, the energy and the efficiency are consistent with one another**, which is worth stating because several other published quantities in this programme are not.

**Every aerodynamic gain in this aeroplane exists to be spent against that ratio.** The smaller wing, the higher aspect ratio, the wingtip propellers and the blown leading edge are all attempts to buy back efficiency in an airframe carrying an energy source two orders of magnitude worse than the one it replaced.

## The Thing That Actually Stopped It

**The X-57 was not stopped by its wing, its battery or its aerodynamics. It was stopped by transistors.**

Converting stored direct current into the alternating current a motor wants is the business of power electronics, and it is a mature discipline with a large literature [[Mohan and Robbins, Power electronics][book_mohan]]. The cruise and high-lift motor controllers both used silicon carbide metal oxide semiconductor field effect transistors to convert the battery's direct current into the three-phase alternating current the motors need [[X-57 Cruise Motor and Inverter Failure Modes][research_x57_cruise_motor_inverter_failures]] [[MOSFET][ref_mosfet]] [[Silicon Carbide][ref_sic]] [[Power Inverter][ref_inverter]].

$$
V_{dc} = 538, \qquad I_{pk} = 200
$$

The cruise motor controller switched at 538 volts and 200 amperes peak per phase. **The typical failure mode is a short circuit with material ejection, which usually destroys the drive circuit and the printed circuit board with it.**

**Three separate causes were found and each is mundane.**

**Vibration.** The initial cruise motor controller module could not survive its vibration test, a random profile of 7.7 g root mean square for twenty minutes per axis. It failed by short circuit across the direct current bus. The tests were run at ten kilowatts or less, and the report notes that at higher power the case would probably not have contained the failure.

**Heat.** The thermal gap pad between a high-lift controller transistor and its heat sink was too thin. Imperfections in the transistor case wore through the pad, the junction overheated, and the internals were ejected out of the case.

**Electromagnetic interference was a fourth and separate problem**, discovered late because the controller redesign delayed the on-aircraft testing that would have found it [[Electromagnetic Interference][ref_emi]].

**Software.** An early high-lift controller software version commanded shoot-through, which is both transistors in a half bridge conducting at once and shorting the battery across itself [[Half-Bridge Converter][ref_shoot_through]]. On the cruise side, improper control law tuning destroyed all three phases.

**The deck summarising these failures for a standards committee contains the sentence that best describes the whole programme.** Under assembly, it reads that one loose washer can cause catastrophic failure.

## Why the Programme Was Surprised

**The lessons learned report is unusually direct about the root cause, and it is not a technical one** [[X-57 Maxwell Lessons Learned Report][research_x57_lessons_learned]] [[X-57 Systems Engineering Lessons Learned][research_x57_systems_engineering_lessons]].

**The project was scoped as an integration effort rather than a development effort.** The initial technology readiness assessment concluded that the subsystems existed and could be bought, so the plan assumed that funding and time were not needed to develop them [[Technology Readiness Level][ref_trl]] [[Technology Readiness Level, NASA][ref_nasa_trl]]. **The team was selected to integrate a distributed electric propulsion wing, not to develop the components that make one.** Because no subsystem development was expected, few subsystem risks were identified, and because few risks were identified, no reserves were held against them.

**And then a single direction invalidated the assessment without triggering a new one.**

The readiness assessment rested in part on the existence of, and assumed ability to access, overseas flight-proven hardware. **The direction from senior leadership was to use technology available from United States industry only.** The report states plainly that when that direction was given, the project may have benefitted from reassessing the readiness of American components, and that it did not.

**During execution the actual readiness proved significantly lower than assumed.** The cruise motors, the cruise motor controllers, the battery control modules and the high-lift motor controllers all had to be redesigned. A programme staffed for integration found itself developing four subsystems, first one after another and then in parallel, which raised the technical risk further and delayed the subsystems that had been ready.

**The report's own summary of the consequence is that integration and test of the mature subsystems was delayed**, which increased the probability of cost and schedule impacts arriving late in the project, at a point when there was no schedule and no funding left to absorb them.

## What the Register Recorded and What It Could Not

**The register's entry is a specification, and specifications are written before things are built.**

Every clause of it is accurate as a description of the design. There were smaller electric motors across the wing leading edge acting as a high lift device. There were larger motors mounted on each wing tip for cruise. **The wing was designed for that configuration, and this article has spent several sections showing how tightly it was designed for it.**

**What the entry cannot record is readiness.** A designation register records what a vehicle is for. It has no column for whether the parts exist at the maturity the plan assumed, and that is the quantity on which this programme turned.

**And the length of the entry is, on reflection, evidence of the same thing.** The X-57 has the most detailed mission statement of any X designation because by 2016 the architecture was fully worked out on paper. **The confidence that produced a 343 character specification is the same confidence that produced a technology readiness assessment describing an integration task.**

## The Source Base

**The survey behind this article rests on 3,060 curated records.** Two sweeps of Crossref, the NASA Technical Reports Server and the Defense Technical Information Center retrieved 8,079 records, of which 7,031 were distinct. A homonym store removed 689 of them, being 9.8 percent, and a subject gate kept 3,138 of the remainder. Deduplication removed a further 118.

### The Aeroplane Is Named After a Physicist, and the Store Feels It

**Armed exactly as written the store would remove 830 of the 7,031 records, being 11.8 percent, which is the largest exposure any article in this series has met.** With the one family named below opened it removes 689, being 9.8 percent.

**Some of that is the name.** The aeroplane is named after James Clerk Maxwell [[James Clerk Maxwell][ref_maxwell]], whose surname is attached to a set of equations, a velocity distribution, a viscoelastic material model and a polarization theory [[Maxwell's Equations][ref_maxwell_equations]] [[Maxwell-Boltzmann Distribution][ref_maxwell_distribution]]. The store's geophysics family caught the Love numbers of a generalized Maxwell sphere and a Maxwell-Wagner theory of membrane polarization in shaly sands, and its teaching family caught a study of digital approaches to teaching the Maxwell distribution.

**Of 126 harvested titles containing the word Maxwell, 26 also mention aircraft, propulsion or flight and 100 do not.** Naming an aeroplane after a physicist is a decision with consequences for anybody who later tries to survey it.

### One Family Had to Be Tagged Before It Could Be Opened

**The store carried a pattern removing the rotor of an electrical machine, and it removed 172 records from this survey.** It was added by the X-50 article, where the rotor of an electrical machine was a contaminant of a rotorcraft survey [[X-Planes: Boeing X-50 Dragonfly][related_post_a347_boeing_x50]]. **Here the electrical machine is the subject**, and one of this article's own primary documents is a finite element electromagnetic model of a permanent magnet synchronous cruise motor [[X-57 60kW Permanent Magnet Synchronous Cruise Motor Finite Element Electromagnetic Modeling][research_x57_cruise_motor_electromagnetic]].

**The pattern carried no tag, so there was no handle to open it with.** A tag was added and the family opened, which is the only change this article made to the store's contents rather than to its guards.

### A Venue That Names Four Fields Is Evidence for None of Them

**The store matches the title and the venue together, which is right.** A paper in a marine journal is probably marine even if its title is coy. **It stops being right when the venue is deliberately multi-modal.**

The conference proceedings titled for electrical systems in aircraft, railways, ship propulsion and road vehicles is a principal venue for electric aircraft propulsion. **Its name alone removed 31 records from this survey**, including one titled advanced aircraft electrical systems to enable an all-electric aircraft, whose own title contains no marine or rail word at all. **The venue named this article's subject first and three other subjects afterwards, and the store read only the other three.**

The marine, rail and road families are now guarded against that construction, by a condition that fires only when a string names aircraft alongside another transport mode.

**Opening that one family returned 141 records to the pool.** The histogram above sums to 172, and opening those families returns 141. The difference is 31 records, and it is larger here than in any previous article because the electrical machine pattern overlaps heavily with the marine and wind energy families. **A tag count is a count of first reasons, not a count of releasable records**, so the figure quoted is measured as a set difference rather than summed [[X-Planes: Lockheed Martin X-56][related_post_a353_lockheed_martin_x56]].

### Primary Documents

**342 of the 3,060 curated records are technical reports rather than journal or conference literature**, being 11.2 percent. That measure counts an address at the NASA Technical Reports Server or a Defense Technical Information Center identifier, so it measures where a document is hosted rather than what it is.

### A Programme's Own Index Cannot Be Searched, Only Read

**NASA published a page listing the X-57 programme's technical output.** Sixty-three entries were transcribed from it and sixty-one resolved against the report server, the other two carrying metadata too thin to identify [[X-57 Technical Papers, NASA Aeronautics Research Mission Directorate][ref_nasa_x57_papers]].

**The first sweep, which queried the designation alone, had retrieved eleven of those sixty-one.** That is eighteen percent of the programme's own published output, from a sweep built specifically to avoid the failure the previous article documented [[X-Planes: Lockheed Martin X-56][related_post_a353_lockheed_martin_x56]].

**The reason is not query dilution and not a row cap, and it was tested.** A direct query for the designation reports seventy-four matching records and returns ten. Asking for a hundred returns ten. **Asking for the eleventh onward, by offset, returns the same ten again**, at every offset tried from ten to seventy. The offset is ignored by the endpoint, so the remaining sixty-four records cannot be reached by that interface at all.

**A hypothesis was tested here and disproved.** The suspicion was that the fetching library never paginates and was leaving records on the table, which would have been a defect affecting every article in this series. **It paginates correctly and the server ignores it.** The library's own note already said the endpoint caps well below what is asked for, and that note is right.

**So the previous article's lesson needs strengthening rather than repeating.** A353 concluded that a distinctive designation must be queried alone rather than beside generic words. **This article did that and still retrieved eighteen percent**, because the constraint is not the query. **A programme's own published index must be read. It cannot be searched.**

**61 of them were curated by hand from that list**, which NASA maintains as a page of technical papers rather than as a bibliography inside any one report [[X-57 Technical Papers, NASA Aeronautics Research Mission Directorate][ref_nasa_x57_papers]]. **Reading it early is a lesson taken from the previous article**, whose sweep missed sixteen of the seventeen documents its subject's own flight report cited [[X-Planes: Lockheed Martin X-56][related_post_a353_lockheed_martin_x56]].

### The Age of the Literature

**The median year of the curated records is 2018.** 1,717 of them, being 59 percent, were published in 2015 or later, and 295 predate 1980. **A survey of an instability first understood in the 1930s that is three quarters contemporary is telling you that the control problem is the live one**, because the aerodynamics has not changed and the actuators and the computers have.

### What the Gate Was Built For, and What It Refuses

**The gate is A350's gate reordered.** That article was about a wing twisted on purpose, and its own comment on its flutter block called flutter the dynamic problem the flight programme deliberately dropped [[X-Planes: Boeing X-53 Active Aeroelastic Wing][related_post_a350_boeing_x53]]. **Here the dropped problem is the keystone**, and the block that stood as a boundary term there is promoted to the front. Nothing from the X-55 article survives, because a shared contractor is not a shared subject [[X-Planes: Lockheed Martin X-55 ACCA][related_post_a352_lockheed_martin_x55]].

**And it names the vehicles explicitly, which it did not at first.** The publication review found that the Helios mishap findings, a primary document this article's argument rests on, were retrieved by the first sweep and then refused by the gate for carrying no subject anchor. Its title names a vehicle and an event and no physics at all. **That is the same defect the sweep had, one stage later.** The sweep could not find the aeroplane's own literature because a bibliographic query dilutes a designation with its generic neighbours, and the gate could not keep it because a gate reads for subject words that a vehicle name does not contain. **A proper noun is the one thing a subject gate is blind to**, so the proper nouns are now named in it.

**It refuses hypersonics deliberately.** Panel flutter under piston theory is a real instability and a different regime from a 120 knot flying wing, and this series treats it elsewhere. It refuses missiles, marine vehicles, surface transport and turbomachinery for the same reason, which is that each has a flutter literature of its own that would swamp this one.

**The pool divides into 15 clusters and the largest is not the aeroplane.** It is the flutter literature itself.

| Cluster | Records |
|---|---:|
| Distributed electric propulsion, which is the thing the register names | 388 |
| Blowing a wing so that it can be smaller | 209 |
| Propellers at the wingtip, and the vortex they turn against | 40 |
| The battery, which is the constraint the whole architecture is built around | 443 |
| Motors, inverters and the switching devices that ended this programme | 249 |
| Keeping it cool, which a piston engine does with its own exhaust | 56 |
| Electric aeroplanes as a class, and whether the sums work | 352 |
| Propeller theory, which is older than any of this | 196 |
| The drag the smaller wing was meant to save | 126 |
| Lift, stall and the wing that had to be made smaller | 321 |
| Sizing an aeroplane around a new kind of engine | 115 |
| Proving it is safe, which is where this one stopped | 62 |
| Measuring it, on the ground and in the air | 87 |
| The atmosphere and the flight condition | 46 |
| Other aeronautical, propulsion and electrical literature | 370 |
| **Total** | **3,060** |

## What the Data Changed

**The equation pass tried to verify the central claim and could not.** The draft asserted that twelve propellers supply a factor of 2.38 in lift, which is a computation the published numbers permit. **Treating the propellers as ideal actuator discs puts the required power at 139 kilowatts against 151 installed**, and an ideal disc has no profile drag, no swirl, no tip loss and no motor loss. **At a realistic propulsive efficiency the requirement exceeds the installed power.** That is a boundary on the explanation rather than a fault in the aeroplane. **Raising the dynamic pressure over seventy percent of the span cannot by itself account for landing this wing**, so the slipstream must be doing more than speeding the air up, which is what the blown-wing literature says it does.

**And the published range does close, which is worth stating because several other published quantities do not.** The stored energy, the drivetrain efficiency and the weight together imply a lift to drag ratio of about seventeen, which is a reasonable figure for a clean aeroplane of this aspect ratio.

**Working the power density showed the motors to be a smaller advance than the architecture suggests.** A cruise motor with its propeller gives 1.36 kilowatts per kilogram against about 1.17 for the piston engine it replaced, and that comparison already flatters the motor. **What distributed electric propulsion buys is not power density but divisibility**, because an electric motor loses very little efficiency as it is made smaller and a piston engine loses a great deal.

**The primary pass found that a programme's own index cannot be searched, only read.** NASA publishes a page listing the X-57 programme's technical output, and sixty-three entries transcribed from it resolved to sixty-one records. **The first sweep, which queried the designation alone, had retrieved eleven of them.** That is eighteen percent, from a sweep built specifically to avoid the failure the previous article documented.

**The reason was tested rather than assumed.** A direct query for the designation reports seventy-four matching records and returns ten. Asking for a hundred returns ten. **Asking for the eleventh onward by offset returns the same ten again**, at every offset from ten to seventy. **The suspicion was that the fetching library never paginates, which would have been a defect affecting every article in this series. It paginates correctly and the server ignores it.** The hypothesis was disproved by the check that was written to confirm it.

**So the previous article's lesson needs strengthening rather than repeating.** Query a distinctive designation alone rather than beside generic words, yes. **But this article did that and still retrieved eighteen percent, because the constraint is not the query.** A programme's own published index must be read.

**A check on the published geometry found that it does not close.** The flight performance report gives a wing area, a span and an aspect ratio for the same wing, and any two of those determine the third. For the original wing they agree to better than one percent. **For the new wing they disagree by 6.4 percent.** The article now uses the two quantities that enter its computations and states that it is not using the third.

**A tempting opening was tested against the register and abandoned.** The engines cell for this row is empty, which invites the reading that the register had no category for an aeroplane without engines. **Eighty-eight rows have an empty engines cell, and the two other X rows that do are a supersonic demonstrator and a rocket.** The blank is a gap in the compilation.

**The induced drag result was derived before the primary source was found to state it.** Working from the published spans, a shorter span at higher aspect ratio gives 32 percent more induced drag at the same lift and speed. The flight performance report says the same thing in prose. **Agreement between an independent derivation and the source is worth more than either alone**, and it is recorded here as a check that passed rather than as a discovery.

**The sweep was built the way the previous article's failure taught it to be built.** A353 found that naming its aeroplane once, alongside five generic words, spent the entire query on the generic words and returned a row cap of tiltrotor testbeds. **Every proper noun in this harvest was queried alone**, and the aeroplane's own literature came back with it.

**The store met its largest exposure yet in this series, and one family had to be tagged before it could be opened.** The pattern removing the rotor of an electrical machine was added by A347, where an electrical rotor contaminated a rotorcraft survey. **Here the electrical machine is the subject**, and one of the article's own primary documents is an electromagnetic model of a permanent magnet synchronous cruise motor. The pattern carried no tag, so there was no handle to open it with, and one was added.

**And a venue that names four transport modes was rejecting papers about the first of them.** The conference proceedings titled for electrical systems in aircraft, railways, ship propulsion and road vehicles is a principal venue for exactly this subject. **The store joins the title and the venue before matching**, which is right when a venue carries evidence, so the venue name alone removed 31 records including one whose title reads advanced aircraft electrical systems to enable an all-electric aircraft. **A venue naming several fields is evidence for none of them**, and the marine, rail and road families are now guarded against that construction.

## Epistemic State

**Facts.** The register's wording and the counts taken across its 526 rows, the geometry, masses, powers and energies attributed to the flight performance report and the traction power documents, and the failure modes attributed to the inverter deck, are quoted from those documents, and every arithmetic relation stated here has been recomputed rather than repeated.

**Inference.** That the length of the register entry reflects the maturity of the paper design is an inference. It is consistent with the entry being a specification rather than a purpose clause, and with the readiness assessment made in the same period, but the register does not say why the entry is long.

**Hypothesis.** That the buy-American direction is the proximate cause of the readiness mismatch is close to what the lessons learned report says but slightly stronger than it. **The report says the project may have benefitted from reassessing the readiness of American components and that the actual readiness proved significantly lower.** It does not assert that the direction caused the mismatch, and neither does this article.

**Unresolved.** The high-lift motor power is given as 12.6 kilowatts by the flight performance report and as 10.5 kilowatts by the reference literature. This article uses the report's figure and records the disagreement. The battery's specific energy is quoted as 80 watt hours per pound, while the usable energy divided by the pack mass gives 55, and the difference between a cell rating and a usable pack figure is not documented in the sources consulted here.

## Out of Scope

**Whether battery aviation is a good idea is out of scope.** This article computes what this aeroplane's numbers were and does not extrapolate them to a technology.

**The certification consequences are in the reference survey but not in the argument.** The programme published a certification gap analysis and an airworthiness validation plan [[Certification Gap Analysis][research_x57_certification_gap_analysis]] [[X-57 Maxwell Airworthiness Validation Plan][research_x57_airworthiness_validation_plan]], and what a normal category aeroplane has to prove is written down [[14 CFR Part 23, Airworthiness Standards for Normal Category Aeroplanes][ref_part23]]. Those belong to a different article, and the programme's own view of what it left behind is published [[X-57 Project Creates Paths Toward Electric Aviation, NASA][ref_nasa_x57_paths]].

## Conclusion

**The register gave this aeroplane the most detailed entry of any X designation, and the aeroplane never flew.**

The entry is not wrong. **The wing was designed optimally for the electric motor configuration**, and the design is tight enough that its parts can only be understood together. The wing was cut to 42 percent of its area to reduce drag in cruise. That made it unable to land, so twelve propellers were put along its leading edge to blow it. Those propellers add wetted area, so the parasite saving fell from 58 percent to 18. The span came down as the aspect ratio went up, so the induced drag rose by 32 percent. **The cruise propellers went to the wingtips because after all of that the configuration needed the vortex energy back.**

**And underneath every one of those decisions is a battery carrying ninety-nine times less energy per kilogram than the fuel it replaced**, giving back a factor of three in drivetrain efficiency and leaving a factor of thirty-two to be found somewhere in the aerodynamics.

**None of that is what stopped it.** What stopped it was that the transistors in the motor controllers failed under vibration, failed under heat, and failed when the software commanded both halves of a bridge to conduct at once. **The programme had been scoped on the assumption that those controllers could be bought rather than developed**, and the assessment that produced that assumption was never repeated after the sourcing direction that invalidated it.

**A designation register has a column for the contractor, the sponsor, the engines and the mission. It has no column for whether the parts exist yet.** The X-57's entry is the longest in its class because the design was finished. **The design being finished is not the same as the aeroplane being possible**, and it is the only one of those two things a register can record.
